   viscosity = 1e-6  # Fluid viscosity\r\n        radius = diameter / 2\r\n        area = np.pi * radius ** 2\r\n        velocity = flowrate / area\r\n        headloss = (128 * viscosity * length * A Multivariate Statistical Framework for Detection, Classification and Pre-localization of Anomalies in Water Distribution Networks\nL14: #  A Multivariate Statistical Framework for Detection, Classification and Pre-localization of Anomalies in Water Distribution Networks\nL15: \nL16: cite12†Image: Open Access L17: \nL18: Authors\nL19: \nL20:   * Oleg Melnikov\nL21:   * Yurii Dorofieiev\nL22:   * Yurii Shakhnovskiy\nL23:   * Huy Truong\nL24: \nL25: Show more\nL26: \nL27:   * cite3†Victoria Degeler cite13†Image: ORCID logo†www.dare.uva.nl L28:   * Show less\nL29: \nL30: Publication date 01-06-2026\nL31: Journal Expert Systems With Applications\nL32: Article number 131450\nL33: Volume | Issue number 313\nL34: Number of pages 21\nL35: Organisations\nL36:   * Faculty of Science (FNWI) - Informatics Institute (IVI)\nL37: \nL38: Abstract\nL39: This paper presents a unified framework for the detection, classification, and preliminary localization of anomalies in water distribution networks using multivariate statistical analysis. The approach, termed SICAMS (Statistical Identification and Classification of Anomalies in Mahalanobis Space), processes heterogeneous pressure and flow sensor data through a whitening transformation to eliminate spatial correlations among measurements.\nL40: Based on the transformed data, the Hotelling’s T2 statistic is constructed, enabling the formulation of anomaly detection as a statistical hypothesis test of network conformity to normal operating conditions. It is shown that Hotelling’s T2 statistic can serve as an integral indicator of the overall “health” of the system, exhibiting correlation with total leakage volume, and thereby enabling approximate estimation of water losses via a regression model.\nL41: A heuristic algorithm is developed to analyze the T2 time series and classify detected anomalies into abrupt leaks, incipient leaks, and sensor malfunctions. Furthermore, a coarse leak localization method is proposed, which ranks sensors according to their statistical contribution and employs Laplacian interpolation to approximate the affected region within the network.\nL42: Application of the proposed framework to the BattLeDIM L-Town benchmark dataset demonstrates high sensitivity and reliability in leak detection, maintaining robust performance even under multiple leaks. These capabilities make the method applicable to real-world operational environments without the need for a calibrated hydraulic model.\nL43: Show more\nL44: Document type Article\nL45: Language English\nL46: Published at cite4†https://doi.org/10.1016/j.eswa.2026.131450†doi.org L47: Downloads\nL48: \nL49: cite5†A Multivariate Statistical Framework for Detection, Classification and Pre-localization of Anomalies in Water Distribution Networks†pure.uva.nl (Final published version)\nL50: \nL51: cite6†Permalink to this page†hdl.handle.net L52: \nL53: cite1†Back L54:   * cite7† University library†uba.uva.nl L55:   * cite8† Disclaimer L56:   * cite9† Terms of Use L57:   * cite10† Contact†uba.uva.nl L58:   * cite11† About UvA-DARE†uba.uva.nl L59: \nL60: Copyright UvA 2026\nL61: \nL62:  To top\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nInternal Error ()\nciteturn29view1 [wordlim: 200] Source: open({\"ref_id\":\"https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S259012302502969X\",\"lineno\":null}); Total lines: 1\nL0: Failed to fetch https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S259012302502969X: (403) Forbidden\n"

Polycarpou\r\nKIOS Research and Innovation Center of Excellence, University of Cyprus, Cyprus\r\n1. INTRODUCTION\r\nDrinking Water Distribution Networks (DWDN) are susceptible to infrastructure failures, which may lead to\r\nwater losses. Typically, these water losses are due to background leakages and pipe bursts which may occur\r\nanywhere within the distribution network. Background leakages are normally difficult to detect due to their\r\nsmall size, whereas pipe bursts are easier to locate as they are of larger size and may appear on the surface.\r\nThe early detection and localization of some leakage event is extremely important, as this would reduce the\r\ntime required for accommodating the event and therefore reducing the risk of further infrastructure degrada-\r\ntion, contamination events and consumer complaints. In previous years, a number of methodologies have\r\nbeen proposed to detect and isolate the location of leakage events using various types of sensor measure-\r\nments. These methods were commonly evaluated on private commercial datasets, and as a result, it is not\r\npossible to objectively compare these methods in their ability to detect and isolate leaks. In the past year, a\r\nleakage detection dataset has been proposed, LeakDB [1], based on benchmark networks and created using\r\nthe WNTR tool [2], using pressure-driven demands and realistic leakage modelling [3]. Inspired by the “BATtle\r\nof the Attack Detection ALgorithms” (BATADAL), which focused on the detection of cyber-physical attacks [4],\r\nour team decided to organize a similar “battle” focusing on leakage events.\r\nThe Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods (BattLeDIM), organized as part of the 2nd Inter-\r\nnational CCWI/WDSA Joint Conference in Beijing, China (http://www.ccwi-wdsa2020.com/), aims at objec-\r\ntively comparing the performance of methods for the detection and localization of leakage events, relying on\r\nSCADA measurements of flow and pressure sensors installed within water distribution networks. Participants\r\nmay use different types of tools and methods, including (but not limited to) engineering judgement, machine\r\nlearning, statistical methods, signal processing, and model-based fault diagnosis approaches.\r\nPAGE 2\r\n2. CHALLENGE DESCRIPTION\r\nL-Town (see Fig. 1) is a small hypothetical town with a population of around 10,000 people. The water utility\r\nof L-Town is responsible for delivering drinking water to consumers through a network of pipes with a total\r\nlength of 42.6km. In previous years, the utility was experiencing a large number of pipe breaks and water\r\nlosses, affecting its service quality. The water distribution network of L-Town is receiving water from two (2)\r\nreservoirs, and the water utility aims at providing water with a pressure head of at least 20m to all of its\r\nconsumers. A Pressure Reduction Valve (PRV) is installed in the lower part of the town (“Area B”), to help\r\nreducing background leakages. PRVs are also installed downstream of the two main reservoirs, to help regu-\r\nlating the pressure. A pump and a water tank have been installed in the higher part of the town (“Area C”), to\r\nprovide sufficient pressure to the consumers of that area. The tank has a diameter of 16 meters with a cylin-\r\ndrical shape. The pump has been programmed so that the tank should be refilling during the night and emp-\r\ntying to “Area C” during the day. There are three consumer types in L-Town: residential, commercial and in-\r\ndustrial. During workdays (Monday to Friday), water consumption follows a similar pattern, whereas during\r\nthe weekend (Saturday and Sunday), there is higher consumption during late hours as the result of night life.\r\nAreas with industrial users do not follow the same pattern of consumption. L-Town is located in the Northern\r\nhemisphere, thus higher water usage is expected around July/August, and lower in December/January. For\r\nthe purposes of this challenge, there are no significant variations of water consumption during holidays or\r\nother special days.\r\n\r\nFigure 1: The L-Town Benchmark Network\r\nTo enhance its capability in monitoring water losses, the water utility of L-Town decided to install one (1) tank\r\nwater level sensor, three (3) flow sensors, and 33 pressure sensors (see Fig. 2), all transmitting their measure-\r\nments every 5 minutes to the utility’s Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (SCADA) System. Pressure\r\nsensors give an average value of the last 5 minutes, which mitigates the uncertainty due to pressure transients\r\nPAGE 3\r\nin the system. In addition, 82 Automated Metered Readings (AMRs) have been installed in “Area C”, for deliv-\r\nering water consumption data directly to the SCADA system (see Fig. 3). Each AMR gives the aggregated con-\r\nsumption of many users in the AMR area.\r\nDuring 2018, a number of leakage events occurred, which were detected and fixed by the water utility. How-\r\never, it is believed that a number of smaller leakages occurred but not revealed. It is also assumed that some\r\nleakages occurred abruptly, whereas others developed gradually, as incipient events, from background leaks\r\ninto pipe bursts.\r\nTo assist the L-Town water utility decision-making process, the utility developed an EPANET-based nominal\r\nmodel of the distribution network, in which base demands were assigned to nodes, following historical data\r\nof proximity consumers. Moreover, two nominal demand patterns were identified for residential and com-\r\nmercial consumer types (with some discrepancies). The utility believes that there might be some inaccuracies\r\nin the model, e.g., with respect to the pipe roughness and pipe diameters. In addition, the utility was not able\r\nto confirm the status of all the valves in the network (i.e., whether they are open or closed).\r\nThe L-Town water utility is searching for a solution to help them analyzing the SCADA dataset, and detect\r\nleakage events as fast as possible. In addition, it is crucial for the utility to have an indication where approxi-\r\nmately the leakage occurs, so that the field workers can inspect those potential leaks using their equipment.\r\n\r\nFigure 2: The locations of the 33 pressure sensors placed in L-Town are indicated with yellow color along with their node ID.\r\nPAGE 4\r\nFigure 3: 82 AMR locations (nodes with red color) in “Area C” of L-Town. Nodes colored in blue do not have AMRs installed.\r\n3. COMPETITION GOAL\r\nThe L-Town utility has created an open call for teams to demonstrate their ability in detecting and localizing\r\nleakage events. The teams will be given a historical SCADA dataset along with information related with the\r\nleakages detected and fixed by the utility throughout 2018, to use for training purposes and for calibrating\r\ntheir models. It is possible that more leakage events occurred during 2018, however the utility was not able\r\nto detect and localize them.\r\nThroughout 2019, the utility conducted periodic surveys using additional sensing equipment, pipe inspections\r\nand other methods, and was able to detect and isolate all the leakage events that occurred within that period.\r\nThe most critical of these events were repaired, however it was not possible to repair some of these leakages\r\ndue to financial reasons.\r\nThe overall goal of this competition, is to identify methods which are able to detect and localize the leakage\r\nevents that occurred in L-Town in 2019, as fast as possible (with respect to time) and as accurately as possible\r\n(with respect to their location), in order to minimize their overall financial costs, both in water losses, as well\r\nas due to the hours spent in isolating the leakage by the utility staff. The L-Town utility will compare the dif-\r\nferent solutions and select the best one based on that objective. More details regarding the evaluation proce-\r\ndure will be released in January 2020.\r\n4. DATASET\r\nThe participant will be provided with the following data:\r\n1. “2018 SCADA.xlsx” historical dataset (also in CSV): The historical data have been collected for the period\r\n2018-01-01 00:00 until 2018-12-31 23:55, at 5-minute time steps The SCADA dataset is comprised of the\r\nwater tank level, the flow sensors, the AMR measurements and the pressure sensors.\r\n2. “2018 Leak Report.txt” (TXT): The repair times of pipe bursts that have been fixed are provided.\r\n3. “L-Town.inp” EPANET Model (INP): A model of the network is provided with nominal parameters for all the\r\nsystem elements. The nominal base demands for each node are based on average historical metered con-\r\nsumption. In general, the difference between the actual and the nominal values for each consumer type\r\n(residential and commercial) is less than 10%. Weekly demand profiles for two consumer types (residential\r\nand commercial) are also provided, however they do not capture the yearly seasonality. Furthermore, the\r\nEPANET model parameters may be different from the actual network parameters (e.g., diameters, rough-\r\nness coefficients), and it is assumed than in general this difference is no greater than 10% of the nominal\r\nvalues.\r\nPAGE 5\r\n4. “2019 SCADA.xlsx” evaluation Dataset (also in CSV): The evaluation data have been collected for the period\r\n2019-01-01 00:00 until 2019-12-31 23:55, at 5-minute time steps. The SCADA dataset is comprised of the\r\nwater tank level, the flow sensors, the AMR measurements and the pressure sensors. This dataset will be\r\nused for the evaluation of the competing leakage detection methodologies, and will be disclosed within\r\nJanuary 2020.\r\nSensor details:\r\n•\r\nThree (3) flow sensors exist in the system, two at the DMA inflows and one at the pump. evaporating flow - ScienceDirect (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0955799723001376)\nciteturn30search0 [wordlim: 200] Published: 3.4 years ago; Crawled: 5 months ago; The newly developed correlation for pressure drop outperformed the existing correlations resulting in an MAE =19.07%.\n\n## Engineering Analysis with Boundary Elements\n\nVolume 151, June 2023, Pages 519-537\n\n# Deep learning-based assessment of saturated flow boiling heat transfer and two-phase pressure drop for evaporating flow\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.enganabound.2023.03.016Get rights and content\n\n## Abstract\n\nThis work proposes new correlations as well as deep learning based modeling of saturated flow boiling heat transfer and two-phase pressure drops for evaporating flow. First, existing saturation flow boiling heat transfer correlations are compared to experimental database (2,500 data points) of numerous refrigerants for tube diameters ranging from 1 to 7 mm. The newly developed correlation for heat transfer outperformed the existing correlations resulting in an MAE =11.28%. For two-phase pressure drop of the evaporating flow, 1,954 measurement data points of 7 refrigerants were used, for the experimental tube diameter ranged from 0.509 to 14 mm. The newly developed correlation for pressure drop outperformed the existing correlations resulting in an MAE =19.07%. An optimal deep learning model (DL) was developed that further improved the accuracy of the prediction in terms of both heat transfer and pressure drop (R^{2}=0.984 and MAE=4.5 % in terms of heat transfer and R^{2}=0.994 and MAE=7.39 % in terms of two-phase pressure drop). The proposed correlations and deep learning models significantly improve microchannel prediction in terms of heat transfer and two phase pressure drop. Besides, explainable artificial intelligence highlights the dependence and interaction between various features affecting the heat transfer and pressure drop.\n\n## Introduction\n\nBoth heat transfer and two-phase pressure drop remained as the areas of research for decades [1], [2], [3], [4], [5], [6]. This is because these are the main design parameters of heat exchangers, evaporators, condensers and heat pipes. Many energy-saving systems can benefit from accurately predicted saturated flow boiling heat transfer and two-phase friction pressure drop. Under the Kigali amendment of the Montreal Protocol limits [7] for CFC and HCFC refrigerants with high GWP (global warming potential), refrigeration systems must replace their refrigerants with HFO refrigerants with low GWP, such as R1234yf, R1234ze, R513A, etc. Therefore, the heat transfer and friction pressure loss of HFO refrigerants must be accurately predicted.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nDeep Learning and Explainable AI Based Blast Wave Pressure Prediction in IoV Applications - ScienceDirect (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S1877050924034616)\nciteturn30search1 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last month; ## Procedia Computer Science ... # Deep Learning and Explainable AI Based Blast Wave Pressure Prediction in IoV Applications ... This paper presents a novel deep learning model designed for predicting blast frequencies in BLEVE (Boiling Liquid Expanding Vapor Explosion) scenarios. The article evaluates several activation functions, including ReLU, Mish, ELU, Silu, and Leaky ReLU, to determine their effectiveness in improving model accuracy. The results indicate that the Mish activation function achieves slightly lower Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) and Mean Absolute Error (MAE) scores on the test dataset, with Train RMSE at 0.075 and Test RMSE at 0.099. The Silu activation function also demonstrates strong performance, yielding a Train RMSE of 0.074 and Test RMSE of 0.095, alongside high R^{2} scores, indicating a good fit to the data. The paper further explores the interpretability of the model’s predictions using the LIME framework, revealing insights into how sensor positions and vapor heights influence blast frequency predictions. This research explores the potential of deep learning techniques in enhancing safety measures and predictive capabilities in hazardous scenarios, contributing valuable insights to the field of risk assessment and management in IoV applications.\n\n## Keywords\n\nBlast Wave Pressure\n\nDeep Leaning\n\nExplainable Artificial Intelligence\n\nPrediction\n\n## Cited by (0)\n\n© 2025 The Author(s). In the test set evaluation, PSnet predicts wall pressure distributions from geometric parameters with a mean absolute error (MAE) of 0.005, accurately capturing shock locations and pressure gradients.\n\n## Aerospace Science and Technology\n\nVolume 168, Part F, January 2026, 111185\n\n# Cascade neural network model for predicting the flow field of two-dimensional nozzle with data assimilation\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.ast.2025.111185Get rights and content\n\n## Highlights\n\n  * •\n\nA cascade neural network is proposed for aerodynamic field reconstruction of 2D vectoring nozzles.\n\n  * •\n\nThe model effectively integrates sparse experimental pressure data through data assimilation to enhance prediction accuracy.\n\n  * •\n\nThe model demonstrates strong generalization capability even under limited-data conditions and varying geometries.\n\n  * •\n\nThe framework provides a scalable and efficient approach for flow prediction and design support in nozzle engineering.\n\n## Abstract\n\nThis is new, This study presents a cascaded neural network framework for predicting internal flow fields in two-dimensional vectoring nozzles by integrating computational fluid dynamics (CFD) simulations with sparse experimental data. The framework consists of two core models: a one-dimensional convolutional Pressure Scan Network (PSnet) and a U-Net-based Contour Reconstruction Network (CRnet). In the test set evaluation, PSnet predicts wall pressure distributions from geometric parameters with a mean absolute error (MAE) of 0.005, accurately capturing shock locations and pressure gradients. CRnet reconstructs two-dimensional flow contours from wall pressure and geometry-derived features, achieving a mean relative error (MRE) of 0.001 and a structural similarity index (SSIM) of 0.948, thereby demonstrating strong accuracy in recovering detailed flow structures such as shocks and expansion waves. A data assimilation method is further employed to incorporate sparse experimental pressure measurements, reducing prediction errors by up to 34 % on average across the test set. Results from the ablation and robustness studies confirm that the proposed input configuration and training strategy are not only effective but also essential for accurate flow field reconstruction. The aim of this work is to establish a general framework that assists researchers and engineers working on complex configuration design like 2D nozzles in building high-dimensional surrogate models that exploit both CFD and experimental data. To this end, we propose a cascaded neural network framework consisting of two convolution-based models [32]: the Pressure Scan Network (PSnet) and the Flow Contour Reconstruction Network (CRnet). PSnet employs a CNN-based regression scheme with an innovative data assimilation strategy based on mathematical interpolation [33] to predict nozzle wall pressure distributions. By assimilating sparse experimentally measured wall pressure data, the PSnet output can be adaptively corrected, thereby improving prediction accuracy. CRnet, built upon a U-Net architecture, reconstructs flow field contours by integrating pressure distribution information. The encoder–decoder structure of CRnet incorporates two key innovations: (1) a pressure-branch module for feature fusion, and (2) a gradient-weighted loss function that enhances prediction accuracy in high-gradient regions such as shock waves. The cascaded framework takes design parameters (0D) as input, predicts pressure distributions along the nozzle wall (1D), and ultimately reconstructs flow field contours (2D). By progressively leveraging information across stages, the proposed architecture achieves accurate and efficient predictions, and is particularly suitable for downstream optimization tasks requiring surrogate model reuse [34], without further CFD calculation.\n\nThe proposed framework enables the direct prediction of both wall pressure distributions and flow field contours from nozzle design parameters. It supports deformable, multi-condition, three-dimensional configurations during the design phase, and is trained in separate stages: from design parameters to pressure distributions, and subsequently from distributions to contour fields. Moreover, the framework incorporates sparse experimental pressure data via data assimilation, substantially improving the accuracy and robustness of the predictions. This capability allows designers to rapidly infer macroscopic flow characteristics from parameter inputs, providing high-dimensional insights that go beyond conventional low-dimensional metrics [31], [35]. The cascaded structure leverages the implicit knowledge captured in individually supervised stages, thereby enhancing the overall quality of predictions and enabling more comprehensive flow analyses. The remaining sections of the paper are organized as follows: Section 2 presents the three-dimensional parameterized geometry construction method, the CFD process, and the dataset preparation pipeline. Section 3 introduces the underlying principles of each cascade stage, the data assimilation method, and the full data flow during training and inference. Section 4 provides a comparative evaluation of model performance and includes ablation studies [36] examining different input and training configurations. Section 5 summarizes the findings and contributions of this work.\n\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nLedipasvir Plus Sofosbuvir - an overview | ScienceDirect Topics (https://www.sciencedirect.com/topics/medicine-and-dentistry/ledipasvir-plus-sofosbuvir)\nciteturn30search3 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 3 months ago; URL: https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0889855315000667 ... A low mean-squared error, mean absolute error, and a high coefficient of determination were observed when predicting the mean and standard deviation wind pressure coefficients of the full-scale dataset.\nThe proposed ML model combines a few-shot learning model with the existing physical knowledges in the design standards related to the zonal information. This physical information helps in clustering the few-shot learning model and improves prediction performance. Using the proposed techniques, the scaling issue observed in wind tunnel tests can be partially resolved. A low mean-squared error, mean absolute error, and a high coefficient of determination were observed when predicting the mean and standard deviation wind pressure coefficients of the full-scale dataset. In addition, the benefit of incorporating physical knowledge is verified by comparing the results with a baseline few-shot learning model. This method is the first of its type as it is the first time to extrapolate in wind performance prediction by combining prior physical knowledge with a few-shot learning model in the field of wind engineering. With the benefit of the few-shot learning model, only a low-resolution of the measuring tap configuration is required, and the reliance on physical wind tunnel experiments can be reduced. The physics-informed few-shot learning model is an efficient, robust, and accurate alternate solution to predicting wind pressures on full-scale structures based on various modeled scale experiments.\n\n## Keywords\n\nWind tunnel tests\n\n;\n\nFull-scale experiments\n\n;\n\nMachine learning\n\n;\n\nFew-shot learning\n\n;\n\nLow-rise building\n\n## Introduction\n\nWindstorms such as hurricanes, tornadoes, and downbursts are among the most destructive and feared natural hazards. It is a scale-dependent accuracy measurement and cannot be used to make comparisons between data population using different scales. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nA virtual sensor network for pressure distribution inside a multi-zone building based on spatial adjacency relationships and multivariate adaptive regression spline - ScienceDirect (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S2352710223022398)\nciteturn30search8 [wordlim: 200] Published: 2.9 years ago; Crawled: 2 months ago; This study proposes a data-driven method for establishing a virtual pressure sensor network within a multi-zone building. ... All virtual sensors can achieve excellent predicted results with high R^{2} values and low MAE and RMSE, with mean values of 0.997 Pa and 1.269 Pa, respectively.\nThis study proposes a data-driven method for establishing a virtual pressure sensor network within a multi-zone building. Three scenarios with various sample sizes are applied to evaluate their impacts on the analyzed results, and scenario 3 is identified as the most suitable database for creating a virtual sensor network. The single-sided or multi-sided spatial adjacency relationships are determined for each zone, and the MARS model is then applied to develop the relationships among the variables, with key physical sensors designated by the frequency analysis of independent variables. The final sensor network comprises four key physical sensors, thirteen direct virtual sensors, and five indirect virtual sensors. All virtual sensors can achieve excellent predicted results with high R^{2} values and low MAE and RMSE, with mean values of 0.997 Pa and 1.269 Pa, respectively. Notably, the difference in accuracy between direct and indirect virtual sensors is influenced by spatial adjacency relationships. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nPredictive Accuracy - an overview | ScienceDirect Topics (https://www.sciencedirect.com/topics/computer-science/predictive-accuracy)\nciteturn30search9 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last month; URL: https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0003687023000297 ... Mean absolute error (MAE) and root mean squared error (RMSE) are the most commonly used measures to assess predictive accuracy in the environmental sciences (Li and Heap, 2008; Willmott, 1982), despite various advantages and disadvantages associated with them as being discussed in previous studies (Chai and Draxler, 2014; Willmott and Matsuura, 2005, 2006; Willmott et al., 2009).\nSome of these measures were often used as measure of goodness of fit instead of predictive accuracy in previous studies (Gupta et al., 2009; Krause et al., 2005). The key difference is that if the observations used to validate a predictive model were also used for developing the model, the resultant measures are for assessing the goodness of fit of the model; otherwise they are for assessing the predictive accuracy of the model. Consequently, these inconsistences in definitions and applications make the accuracy often incomparable among different studies.\n\nMean absolute error (MAE) and root mean squared error (RMSE) are the most commonly used measures to assess predictive accuracy in the environmental sciences (Li and Heap, 2008; Willmott, 1982), despite various advantages and disadvantages associated with them as being discussed in previous studies (Chai and Draxler, 2014; Willmott and Matsuura, 2005, 2006; Willmott et al., 2009). However their magnitude depends on the scale/unit of the variable predicted (Li and Heap, 2011), which make it impossible to compare the accuracy of predictive models for different disciplines and variables. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nDevelopment of artificial neural networks for the prediction of the pressure field along a horizontal pipe conveying high-viscosity two-phase flow - ScienceDirect (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0955598624000219)\nciteturn30search10 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 3 months ago; Pressure fluctuations are one of the primary concerns regarding the safety of two-phase flows in pipelines. ... Therefore, it is crucial to closely monitor pressure variations and predict their spatiotemporal behavior to the greatest extent possible. In this article, we present the development of a set of models based on artificial neural networks (ANN) to predict the time-dependent behavior of the pressures produced by high-viscosity gas-liquid flows at predetermined locations along a horizontal pipeline. Different volume fractions of glycerin and air constitute the mixtures of interest. The models use the measured values of the mass-flow rates of both fluids at the pipe’s inlet, together with the pressures measured at other locations further downstream. In order to determine the optimal architecture for the artificial neural network, we tested the following four transfer functions for the hidden layer: logsig, tansig, softplus, and swish. Additionally, we utilized the linear function purelin for the output layer. We employed the Levenberg–Marquardt algorithm to train the ANN models and the experimental data set to test their performance.\n\n## Introduction\n\nHigh-viscosity gas-liquid flows are central to a wide range of industrial processes, most notably in the oil and gas, chemical, and food processing industries. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nFrictional pressure drop during steam stratified condensation flow in vacuum horizontal tube (https://www.sciencedirect.com/sdfe/pdf/download/aam/pii/S0017931017324900)\nciteturn30search12 [wordlim: 200] Published: 6.2 years ago; Crawled: 6.2 years ago; The pressure drop in each experimental section was measured by differential pressure transducer. ... In order to verify the accuracy of prediction of each model, two parameters (MRE and MAE) are introduced.MRE is the mean relative error, and MAE is the mean absolute relative error.\nThe pressure drop in each experimental section was measured by differential pressure transducer. Hence, the frictional pressure drop equals to the pressure drop minus the momentum pressure drop, shown in the following equation:\n\n\\[\n\\left(\\frac{\\Delta p}{\\Delta L}\\right)_{\\mathrm{frict}}=\\left(\\frac{\\Delta p}{\\Delta L}\\right)-\\left(\\frac{\\Delta p}{\\Delta L}\\right)_{\\mathrm{mom}}\n\\]\n(30)\n\nIn order to verify the accuracy of prediction of each model, two parameters (MRE and MAE) are introduced. The calculation formulas are shown below:\n\n\\[\nMRE=\\frac{1}{n}\\sum_{i=1}^{n}\\left[\\frac{\\left(\\Delta p/\\Delta L\\right)_{\\mathrm{frict,pred},i}-\\left(\\Delta p/\\Delta L\\right)_{\\mathrm{frict,exp},i}}{\\left(\\Delta p/\\Delta L\\right)_{\\mathrm{frict,exp},i}}\\times100\\%\\right]\n\\]\n(31)\n\n\\[\nMAE=\\frac{1}{n}\\sum_{i=1}^{n}\\left[\\frac{\\left|\\left(\\Delta p/\\Delta L\\right)_{\\mathrm{frict,pred},i}-\\left(\\Delta p/\\Delta L\\right)_{\\mathrm{frict,exp},i}\\right|}{\\left(\\Delta p/\\Delta L\\right)_{\\mathrm{frict,exp},i}}\\times100\\%\\right]\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nCynanchum - an overview | ScienceDirect Topics (https://www.sciencedirect.com/topics/immunology-and-microbiology/cynanchum)\nciteturn30search11 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last month; There are three steps in MAE: 1) separate solutes from sample under the increase of temperature and pressure, 2) diffusion of solvent across the sample, and 3) release the solutes from sample into solvent.\nThere are three steps in MAE: 1) separate solutes from sample under the increase of temperature and pressure, 2) diffusion of solvent across the sample, and 3) release the solutes from sample into solvent.     "text": "Explainable graph neural network for real-time demand states forecasting in water distribution system - ScienceDirect (https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rineng.2025.106911)\nciteturn31search0 [wordlim: 200] Published: 1.1 years ago; Crawled: last month; Volume 27, September 2025, 106911 ... This study innovative proposes an explainable attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) framework for water demand states forecasting from a pressure perspective.\n\n## Results in Engineering\n\nVolume 27, September 2025, 106911\n\n# \n\nResearch paper\n\nExplainable graph neural network for real-time demand states forecasting in water distribution system\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.rineng.2025.106911Get rights and content\n\nUnder a Creative Commons license\n\nOpen access\n\n## Highlights\n\n  * •\n\nAttention-enhanced GNN for real-time demand forecasting based on pressure data.\n\n  * •\n\nModel is independent from hydraulic model, offering broader applicability.\n\n  * •\n\nModel exhibits high spatiotemporal accuracy and sustained long-term effectiveness.\n\n  * •\n\nModel explanation reveals its operational mechanisms, facilitating field deployment.\n\n## Abstract\n\nEfficient real-time nodal demand forecasting in water distribution systems is essential for a reliable and resilient water supply. Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. Pressure sensors provide a more practical alternative. Thus, this study proposes a novel attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) for real-time demand forecasting from a pressure perspective. By integrating attention mechanisms, the AGN overcomes limitations of convolution-based graph neural networks, such as capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic node interactions, leading to enhanced performance on real-world modeling problems. This model is formulated for real and sensitive contexts where, in addition to being accurate, the predictions must also be understandable by human operators. Thus, model-level explanations are then introduced in our explainable framework. The proposed AGN outperforms benchmark methods in both spatiotemporal accuracy and long-term forecasting effectiveness, while simultaneously delivering interpretable insights into the demand forecasting process. The novel approach based on the fusion of forecasting and explainability in a single framework enables the creation of powerful and reliable systems suitable for real-world issues and challenges.\nConsequently, the primary objective of this study focuses on developing an innovative state estimation framework that operates independently of conventional hydraulic modeling paradigms.\n\nSeveral studies [18,33,37] have enabled direct modeling of WDSs, obviating the need for hydraulic models. Based on the assumption of smooth variations in nodal head (the sum of nodal pressure and elevation of a node), Zhou et al. [37] developed a graph-based head reconstruction (GHR) method. This method decomposes the hydraulic head into components with different variation speeds, reconstructing the head using the slowly varying components obtained from a limited number of monitoring nodes. However, this method heavily relies on having a sufficient number of well-defined sensor nodes and a regular underlying graph structure. Xing and Sela [33] were the pioneers in employing a graph neural network (GNN) to achieve state estimation within WDSs. They developed an encoder-decoder GNN architecture, enabling real-time synchronous estimation of pipeline flow and nodal pressure states. These methods are categorized into model-level and instance-level explanations. The former elucidates the behavior and decision-making process of the entire model, while the latter provides input-specific explanations for each graph instance. Model-based explanations offer a macroscopic understanding of the overall model behavior by identifying general patterns and rules under different input scenarios. Within this context, the concept of explainability is tied to specific model fragments. These explanations delve into the inference-making process of the model, providing a comprehensive account of which features significantly impact the inference results [14,28]. These techniques analyze the inference process to determine which features significantly influence outputs, enabling retrospective examination of parameter contributions. Model-level explanations can be further divided into three parts: rule extraction, hidden semantics analysis, and attribution methods [20].\n\nThis study innovative proposes an explainable attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) framework for water demand states forecasting from a pressure perspective. Derived from GRU-based GNN (i.e. GGNN), this model not only exhibits excellent time series prediction performance but also captures the network’s structure. The innovative attention mechanisms are then incorporated to address the limitation of GGNN in capturing solely local dependencies and enhance the model's interpretability through its transparent decision-making process. Overall, the paper encompasses the following novelties:\n\n·Demand forecasting model designed for water network characteristics: A core innovation of this study is the development of the AGN model, specifically designed to capture the unique spatio-temporal characteristics of water distribution networks. The proposed AGN model forecasts future demands at all nodes, without relying on hydraulic models. The AGN model shows significant accuracy in both spatial and temporal dimensions compared to existing direct modeling methods. The model maintains high predictive robustness across various forecasting horizons. Our high accuracy results validate the feasibility and efficiency of demand prediction from a pressure perspective.\n\nModel explanation associated with water network characteristics: Merely achieving sufficient prediction accuracy is not enough to guarantee the model’s deployment in real-world WDSs. To bridge this gap, the second innovation addresses the crucial need for improved model transparency. Specifically, the key lies in integrating model explanations with practical engineering issues, enabling network managers to understand and trust the model, thereby making reliable decisions. To the best knowledge, we are the first to address interpretability issues in WDS. By providing these explanations, this study provided a deeper understanding of the roles of different model components in the prediction task. This process also aids in improving the model by offering feedback to further enhance its predictive performance.\n\n\nBuilding upon the dynamic global weighting capability of attention mechanisms, this study enhances the GGNN framework by integrating self-attention and external-attention modules. The self-attention module addresses the limited ability of standard graph convolutions to capture long-range spatial dependencies among nodes, significantly improving spatial representational capacity. The external-attention module explicitly models the relationships between nodal attributes and pressure observations, enhancing the model's physical relevance to WDSs. Fig. 1 illustrates the overall AGN architecture. The subsequent sections detail the design and implementation of this framework:\n\nImage: Fig 1\n\n  1. The workflow of the proposed AGN model.\n\n(1) Neighbor propagation based on adjacency matrix: By multiplying candidate hidden pressure states (CHPS) 𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 with the weighted adjacency matrix 𝒲𝑣𝑖, the past CHPS of sensor node 𝑣𝑖 is transferred to its first-order neighborhood nodes:((5))̅𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=𝒲𝑣𝑖·𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 where 𝑙 is the iteration of recurrent layers, and the initial CHPS 𝑐(0)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 is an initialized learnable parameter.\n\n(2) Long-short-term memory augmented by self-attention mechanism: By calculating the matrix product of the query vector 𝑄·⁡(·) and the key vector 𝐾·⁡(·) of the node sequence (Eqs. (6)-a and 7-a), the attention between node sequences can be reflected. The attention scores are obtained after normalization through SoftMax activation (Eqs. (6)-b and 7-b). The value vectors 𝑉·⁡(·) of all nodes in the sequence are weighted and summed using the attention scores. 𝑄·⁡(·), 𝐾·⁡(·), and 𝑉·⁡(·) are three different linear transformations. The symbol ∥ denotes the concatenation operation of pressure measurement 𝑝(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 and past CHPS ̅𝑐(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗. 𝐷 is the dimension of the concatenation, preventing gradient explosion.\n\n### 4.2. Results explanation\n\n#### 4.2.1. Rule extraction from recurrent layer\n\nEmploying recurrent graph convolution (as shown in Eq. (5)), the AGN model gradually expands the receptive field of sensor nodes’ neighboring nodes. Simultaneously, pressure feature transfer from sensor nodes to global nodes is achieved by the self-attention mechanism, enabling long-range generalization.\n\nTo understand the rule of recurrent layers and their impact on the model’s generalization ability, this study varied the number of recurrent layers and tested the model’s generalization accuracy under different iterations. Fig. 5(a) shows the relative position relationship between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. The size and color depth of the non-sensor nodes indicate their distance from the nearest sensor nodes. Additionally, the data statistics in the upper right corner reveal the shortest path distribution between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. Notably, most non-sensor nodes are three steps away from sensor nodes, with the longest path being eleven steps. This is primarily due to the attention mechanism’s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodes’ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\n\nIn the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can “perceive” the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. \nExplainable AI and Machine Learning for Water Resources Engineering: Enhancing Decision-Making and Sustainable Development through Interpretable Models in Civil Engineering and Spatial Sciences\n\nEdited by Dr. Komali Kantamaneni (University of Central Lancashire, Preston, , United Kingdom), Professor Upaka Rathnayake (Atlantic Technological University - Sligo, Sligo, , Ireland), Assist. However, this extension was not pursued fur-\r\n1551-ther, as we tried to maintain comparability with the conditions faced by \r\n1552-the BattLeDim participants.\r\n1553-A notable positive correlation was also found between the model \r\n1554-error and the total number of leaks in the system (0.773 for the linear \r\n1555-model and 0.706 for the log-linear model). This ﬁnding suggests that \r\n1556-the increasing structural degradation of the system also contributes to \r\n1557:reduced model accuracy.\r\n1558-Overall, the results indicate that the linear model can provide reli-\r\n1559-able estimates of the total leakage volume in the network, provided that \r\n1560-the deviation from normal operating conditions remains moderate.\r\n1561-6.2.  Unsupervised detection\r\n1562-In practice, it is often challenging for water utilities to obtain a clean \r\n1563:dataset that represents fault-free operating conditions. Historical records \r\n1564-may be contaminated by unrecorded leak events, changes in consump-\r\n1565:tion patterns, or various sensor faults. As a result, it becomes important \r\n1566-to evaluate whether the proposed anomaly detection methodology re-\r\n1567:mains eﬀective in the absence of a dedicated training set.\r\n1568:We compared three strategies for constructing reference (training) \r\n1569-statistics in the absence of labeled leak events:\r\n1570-(i) dropping out a ﬁxed portion of the lowest-pressure observations \r\n1571-for each time cluster. This strategy is motivated by the observation that \r\n1572-leaks lead to the overall loss of pressure in the network (Silva et al., \r\n1573-1996);\r\n1574-(ii) robust covariance estimation using the Minimum Covariance De-\r\n1575-terminant (MCD) with an adjustable support fraction (Hubert et al., \r\n1576:2018), which drops observations that signiﬁcantly diﬀer from the bulk \r\n1577-of the data;\r\n1578-(iii) using all available data without ﬁltering.\r\n1579-The ﬁrst two strategies have yielded similar results. Somewhat un-\r\n1580-expectedly, the third approach has produced results that are, in some \r\n--\n1583-𝐹 statistic for Area A under diﬀerent train-\r\n1584-ing strategies, after applying a moving average and the Wilson–Hilferty \r\n1585-transformation. The true leak count and total leakage volume are shown \r\n1586-for reference. All variables are normalized to a common scale.\r\n1587:As illustrated, the absence of a dedicated training set results in a cer-\r\n1588-tain degradation of performance. The MCD strategy increases the vari-\r\n1589:ance of the test statistic and, consequently, the number of false alarms. \r\n1590:Conversely, using an unﬁltered training set overly smooths the data, \r\n1591-causing some leak events to be missed. Nevertheless, the overall shape \r\n1592-of the time series remains similar, and major leakage events are still \r\n1593-clearly detectable. This demonstrates that the method remains workable \r\n1594-with minimal supervision.\r\n1595:One potential enhancement of unsupervised training methods is to \r\n1596-add some other unsupervised criteria such as temporal consistency to \r\n1597-reﬁne the set of detected outliers.\r\n1598-Expert Systems With Applications 313 (2026) 131450 \r\n1599-18 \r\n--\n1623-interpretable spatial ﬁelds that highlight likely leak regions. In addi-\r\n1624-tion, we designed an event classiﬁcation algorithm that separates stable \r\n1625-and unstable operating periods and distinguishes between abrupt and \r\n1626-incipient leakages.\r\n1627:To address scenarios without fault-free historical data, we evaluated \r\n1628:several unsupervised training strategies. Despite increased variability \r\n1629:and false alarms, major leaks remained clearly detectable, underscoring \r\n1630-the robustness of the approach under minimally supervised conditions.\r\n1631-Future work will focus on combining coarse preliminary localiza-\r\n1632-tion with targeted model-based reﬁnement for precise leak pinpoint-\r\n1633-ing. These developments will explore both hydraulic model-assisted and \r\n1634-purely data-driven strategies, aiming to deliver scalable and adaptable \r\n1635-leak detection tools for operational deployment in modern water distri-\r\n1636:bution systems. In addition, future studies should investigate the trans-\r\n1637-ferability and performance of the proposed framework across diﬀerent \r\n1638-water distribution networks.\r\n1639-Declaration of generative AI and AI-assisted technologies in the \r\n1640-manuscript preparation process\r\n1641-During the preparation of this work the authors used ChatGPT and \r\n1642-Grok in order to improve grammar and style of the text and in order \r\n1643:to create code fragments used in the experiments described in the pa-\r\n1644-per. The maximum of the interpolated ï¬eld always oc-\r\ncurs at one of the support nodes, i.e., the sensor locations. Therefore, \r\nthe described procedure is eï¬€ectively equivalent to verifying whether \r\nthe most out-of-range sensor lies within a 300-meter radius of the leak \r\nposition.\r\nAs expected, higher noise levels make localization more diï¬ƒcult, \r\nwhile increasing the leak magnitude improves it. However, due to the \r\nnature of the method, the localization accuracy does not further improve \r\nonce the leak becomes suï¬ƒciently pronounced. Likewise, the proportion \r\nof leaks that are successfully localized settles at approximately 92%. The \r\nother â‰ˆ8% of instances could be due to the diï¬€erence between geometric \r\ndistance and actual network (hydraulic) distance, potentially impacting \r\nsensor response sensitivity in a non-linear manner.\r\nThe experiment was conducted only for Area A. For Areas B and C, \r\ndue to their small size, detecting a leak within the area is practically \r\nequivalent to localizing it according to the BattLeDIM criteria.\r\nAn example of the successful localization of a single leak is provided \r\nin Fig. 7a, where the scalar ï¬eld indicating the likelihood of leak oc-\r\ncurrence in diï¬€erent network nodes is visualized on the network map \r\nusing a color gradient. The ï¬gure also shows the true leak location, the \r\nestimated leak position, and the 300-meter tolerance radius around the \r\nlatter, in accordance with the BattLeDIM evaluation criteria.\r\nIn the case of multiple simultaneous leaks, the localization task be-\r\ncomes more challenging. When the leaks are spatially well-separated, \r\nthe Laplacian interpolation typically produces a ï¬eld map containing \r\nseveral distinct â€œhot spots.â€ These regions can be identiï¬ed either visu-\r\nally or algorithmically, e.g., by iteratively selecting the sensor with the \r\nhighest deviation and then excluding the corresponding neighborhood \r\nExpert Systems With Applications 313 (2026) 131450 \r\n12 \r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 14 ===\r\nO. Melnikov et al.\r\nFig. 12. Leak detection in area A for 2018 (a) and 2019 (b) SCADA data.\r\nfrom subsequent searches. An example of successful localization using \r\nthis technique is shown in Fig. 7b.\r\nHowever, when leaks are located close to one another or diï¬€er signif-\r\nicantly in size, the smaller leak may be overshadowed by the hydraulic \r\neï¬€ects of the larger one. A representative example is the incipient leak \r\np232. For small discharge rates, it produces only marginal deviations \r\nin the neighboring sensors, which remain dominated by the anomalies \r\ncaused by other, larger leaks, and the leak cannot be correctly localized \r\n(Fig. 7c). As the leak magnitude increases, its contribution becomes suf-\r\nï¬ciently pronounced to be distinguishable even in the presence of three \r\nother leaks (Fig. 7d).\r\nOverall, while iterative suppression of known leaks can enhance \r\nmulti-leak localization in favorable conditions, the reliability of this ap-\r\nproach remains limited. More stable performance can be achieved by \r\nre-training the anomaly detection model on updated data, eï¬€ectively \r\nincorporating detected leaks into a new baseline operating state. Fol-\r\nlowing this, anomaly detection is carried out relative to the revised ref-\r\nerence, allowing for the recognition of incremental changes to the new \r\nstatus quo.\r\nExtending this method to more accurately account for individual sen-\r\nsor contributions, e.g., using the Shapley value (Ghorbani & Zou, 2019), \r\nmay be a promising direction for future work.\r\n5.3.  Discrimination between leaks and faulty sensors\r\nAs an illustrative case for anomaly classiï¬cation, we consider the \r\nproblem of distinguishing between leaks and faulty sensors. To develop \r\na discriminative rule, a simulation study was conducted in WNTR by \r\nintroducing leaks of varying sizes at diï¬€erent nodes within area A of the \r\nL-Town benchmark. To emulate realistic operating conditions, a multi-\r\nplicative Gaussian demand noise of 10% was applied to all nodes. For \r\neach simulated scenario, the number of sensors exceeding the admissible \r\noperational range, ð‘€ð‘Ž, was recorded.\r\nFig. 8 presents the histogram of ð‘€ð‘Ž distribution for diï¬€erent leak \r\nsizes. The results show that the number of out-of-range sensors increases \r\nsharply with leak magnitude. At 1.2 m3/h, the most likely outcome is \r\nthat not a single sensor would exceed its operational range. Leaks of \r\n1.6 m3/h or greater almost invariably cause out-of-range readings in \r\nExpert Systems With Applications 313 (2026) 131450 \r\n13 \r\n\r\n=== PDF PAGE 15 ===\r\nO. Melnikov et al.\r\nFig. 13. Visual localization of the ï¬rst two leaks (p523 and p827) in 2019 dataset.\r\nmultiple sensors, while leaks of greater magnitude are likely to pro-\r\nduce anomalous readings across the entire area. Therefore, if Hotellingâ€™s \r\nð‘‡2 statistic indicates an anomaly but only a single sensor deviates, a \r\nsystematic sensor error is likely. For smaller leaks, which are unlikely \r\nto indicate a pipe burst, deviations in additional sensors are expected \r\nover time; the absence of such corroborating evidence suggests a sensor-\r\nrelated anomaly.\r\nAn alternative approach for distinguishing a leak from a biased \r\nsensor is based on the same idea as the pre-localization method de-\r\nscribed above. For each sensor ð‘—, we compute the normalized devia-\r\ntion ð‘§ð‘—= (ð‘¥ð‘—âˆ’Ì„ð‘¥ð‘—)âˆ•ðœŽð‘—, and evaluate the overall anomaly magnitude as \r\nð‘2 = âˆ‘\r\nð‘—ð‘§2\r\nð‘—. To characterize the spatial distribution of anomalous behav-\r\nior, we quantify the relative contribution of each sensor as ð‘ ð‘—= ð‘§2\r\nð‘—âˆ•ð‘2.\r\nIn scenarios involving a systematic measurement bias, the distribu-\r\ntion of ð‘ ð‘— tends to be highly concentrated at the malfunctioning sensor. \r\nConversely, in the presence of a hydraulic leak, the contributions ð‘ ð‘— ex-\r\nhibit a spatially decaying pattern with increasing distance from the leak \r\nlocation.\r\nFig. 9 demonstrates this behavior by contrasting the distributions of \r\nð‘ ð‘— obtained for a 0.02 m bias applied to sensor n410 and for a 1.9 m3\r\nleak introduced in the adjacent pipe p350. Under the selected simula-\r\ntion conditions, both cases yield comparable values of the Hotellingâ€™s ð‘‡2\r\nstatistic, suï¬ƒciently high to suggest an anomaly. However, the spatial \r\nsignatures of ð‘ ð‘— remain clearly distinguishable, enabling reliable classi-\r\nï¬cation between sensor bias and leak-induced anomalies.\r\n5.4.  Method validation using SCADA data\r\nNext, we examine the application of the proposed leak detection \r\nmethodology to the SCADA dataset used in the BattLeDIM competition. \r\nA summary of the leak events that occurred during the observation pe-\r\nriod is presented in Table A.3.\r\nTable A.3 illustrates that selecting a period of nominal operation for \r\ntraining the model in this benchmark is nontrivial, since the ï¬rst leak \r\noccurred as early as the second week and remained unresolved until \r\nthe ï¬nal period. We therefore consider model training and validation \r\nseparately for each of the three DMAs in L-Town.\r\nThe simplest region to analyze is area B, whose state is monitored by \r\na single sensor, n215. This sensor is largely insensitive to changes in the \r\nrest of the system and exhibits consistently low variance. Consequently, \r\nall time intervals for this sensor were assigned to a single temporal clus-\r\nter. During 2018, area B experienced only one leak event, associated \r\nwith pipe p673. Time intervals outside the leak period were used for \r\nmodel training.\r\nAccordingly, the whitening transformation for area B reduces to \r\nscalar ð‘§-scaling of the form ð‘§ð‘¡= (ð‘¥ð‘¡âˆ’ðœ‡)âˆ•ðœŽ.\r\nThe results of applying the methodology to the training (2018) and \r\nvalidation (2019) sets are presented in Fig. 10. To ensure commensura-\r\nbility, here and below we plot the data using the Wilson-Hilferty cube \r\nroot transformation (ðœ’2(ð‘ )âˆ•ð‘ )1âˆ•3, which is used to symmetrize and ap-\r\nproximately normalize chi-squared distributed variables (Wilson & Hil-\r\nferty, 1931). It distorts the shape of the ð‘2 distribution less than a log-\r\narithmic scale and also avoids negative values.\r\nAs the identiï¬cation relied solely on data from area B, the leak inci-\r\ndent is conï¬ned to the same area.\r\nArea C diï¬€ers from other DMAs in that its water supply is regu-\r\nlated by a storage tank (T1) with a variable water level, controlled \r\nby a pump. This conï¬guration introduces ï¬‚uctuations in the hydraulic \r\nhead, thereby aï¬€ecting the pressure across the network. Consequently, \r\nwe modeled the operational state of area C using a four-dimensional \r\nfeature vector, where three components correspond to pressure sensors \r\n(n1, n4, and n31), and the fourth represents the water level in tank\r\nT1.\r\nIn 2018, area C experienced two leakage events. For training pur-\r\nimport pandas as pd\r\nimport numpy as np\r\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\r\nfrom scipy.stats import f   # Fisher distribution\r\nimport dtutils as dt\r\n\r\n##############################\r\n\r\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\r\n\r\n   # Select all sensors covering area A\r\n   sensors = pd.read_csv('data/sensors.csv') \r\n   sensors = sensors[sensors['Area']=='A']['Name'].tolist()\r\n\r\n   # Main data file\r\n   datafile = pd.read_csv('data/2018_datah.csv', parse_dates=[0], index_col=0)\r\n   evaldata = datafile[sensors]\r\n   eval_map = dt.group_by_hour(evaldata.index)\r\n\r\n   # Setup leakage data & training period. Its moving average\r\n   ax.plot(z_transf.index, fs_ma, color='purple', linewidth=2)\r\n   # Critical values transformed to Wilson-Hilferty scale\r\n   ax.plot([min_time, max_time], [dt.wht(cv95,df1)] * 2, \r\n       color='orange', linestyle='--', label=f'Critical Value @ 95% = {cv95:.2f}')\r\n   ax.plot([min_time, max_time], [dt.wht(cv99,df1)] * 2,\r\n       color='red', linestyle='--', label=f'Critical Value @ 99% = {cv99:.2f}')\r\n\r\n   # Most model-based and mixed model-based/data-driven methods require pressure information from a set of sensors scattered through the inner nodes of the network. Methodology validation Finally, it is interesting to study how the different methods are evaluated, considering that water companies would have great interest on approaches which have been tested on realistic scenarios. The performance assessment of model-based approaches has evolved during the years, as indicated by Table 1. Early methods were tested over synthetic and example networks, leading to solutions that behaved satisfactorily from an academic point of view, but which were not checked to be usable for real scenarios (Pudar and Liggett, 1992; Andersen and Powell, 2000). The trend started changing and real-based case studies were considered, with a majority\r\np.27: 2014; Vrachimis et al., 2021). Mixed model-based/data-driven methods have been mostly tested on real-based networks, although several evaluations over real-world data can be found (Zhang et al., 2016; Soldevila et al., 2017). The focus on synthetic data over real-based WDNs is explained by the common usage of hydraulic models to generate training data, so that the additional generation of testing data was easy (Capelo et al., 2021; Romero et al., 2022). A higher proportion of methods tested with real-world data is found within the data-driven category. This is caused by the abundance of data-driven leak detection strategies (Ye and Fenner, 2011; Wu et al., 2016), which mainly require data from the DMA inflows. This data is normally already gathered by water utilities for control purposes, so the availability of historical datasets is more common. Besides, these techniques are usually trained and calibrated with leak-free data, which is easier to obtain. The output of the decoder is represented as\r\n(ˆyT +1, ˆyT +2, ..., ˆyT +τ)T ∈Rτ\r\net al., 2014], proven efficient in sequence to sequence mod-\r\nelling, is employed in our framework.\r\nThe LSTM unit\r\n[Hochreiter and Schmidhuber, 1997] is used as the basic unit\r\nin both encoder and decoder. A dual-stage attention mech-\r\nanism [Qin et al., 2017] i.e., a spatial attention layer in en-\r\ncoder and a temporal attention layer in decoder, is employed.\r\nSpecifically, a hybrid spatial attention mechanism is proposed\r\nin the first stage. In the second stage, the temporal attention\r\nmechanism is employed.\r\n4.1\r\nSpatial attention in encoder\r\nThe spatial attention mechanism is performed in the first\r\nstage.\r\nFollowing previous work, a dual-stage attention-\r\nbased RNN (DA-RNN)[Qin et al., 2017] serves as an im-\r\nportant baseline in our experiment. Firstly, we present the\r\nattention mechanism employed in DA-RNN. Given Xk =\r\n(xk\r\n1, xk\r\n2, .., xk\r\n3)T ∈RT as k-th input series and he,t−1, se,t−1\r\nas the hidden states and the cell states of the encoder LSTM\r\ncell at step t-1. The spatial attention mechanism can be de-\r\nfined as follows [Qin et al., 2017] [Liang et al., 2018] [Liu et\r\nal., 2019]:\r\nek\r\nt = Ve tanh(We[he,t−1; se,t−1] + UeXk + Be)\r\n(2)\r\nak\r\nt =\r\nexp(ek\r\nt )\r\nPN\r\ni exp(ei\r\nt)\r\n(3)\r\nwhere Ve ∈R1×l, Be ∈Rl We ∈Rl×2m Ue ∈Rl×T are\r\nthe parameters to be learned. l is the hidden dimension of\r\nthe spatial attention module. Specifically, [Qin et al., 2017]\r\nused l = T. m is the dimension of hidden states: he,t−1\r\nand se,t−1. [·; ·] is the concatenation operation. The attention\r\nweights Et = (ek\r\n1, ek\r\n2, ..., ek\r\nt )T will be treated with a softmax\r\nfunction to make sure the sum equals to 1.\r\nIn the aforementioned work, the spatial attention weights\r\nof the k-th sensor ek\r\nt are trained with the series itself across\r\nall time steps Xk ∈RT . The defect of this method is that the\r\npair-wise exogenous correlation is ignored. To overcome this\r\ndefect, we proposed a modified DS-RNN model, namely DS-\r\nRNN-II, which employed a novel spatial mechanism defined\r\nas follows:\r\nek\r\nt = Ve tanh(We[he,t−1; se,t−1] + UeXt + Be)\r\n(4)\r\nwhere Ve ∈R1×h, Be ∈Rh We ∈Rh×2m Ue ∈Rh×n\r\nare the parameters to be learned. h is the hidden dimension\r\nof the spatial attention module. Compared with Function 2,\r\nThe input series Xk is replaced with Xt, which strengthens\r\nthe influence of the input along spatial axes rather than tem-\r\nporal axes. Two spatial attention mechanism (DS-RNN and\r\nDS-RNN-II) will be compared in experiment in the following\r\nsection.\r\nCombining the advantages of the two aforementioned spa-\r\ntial attention mechanism, we further develop a hybrid spatial\r\nattention mechanism. The model proposed is called hybrid\r\ndual-stage attention based recurrent networks (hDA-RNN).\r\nAs shown in Function 5, the proposed hybrid attention mech-\r\nanism employed both Xk and Xt to train the spatial atten-\r\ntion weights. Considering both the k-th series over a window\r\nlength of T Xk and the exogenous series at current step t,\r\nthe attention weight is more suitable. The hybrid attention\r\nmechanism is defined as follows:\r\nek\r\nt = Ve tanh(We[he,t−1; se,t−1]+UeXk+U ′\r\neXt+Be) (5)\r\nak\r\nt =\r\nexp(ek\r\nt )\r\nPN\r\ni exp(ei\r\nt)\r\n(6)\r\nwhere Ve ∈R1×h, Be ∈Rh We, Le ∈Rh×2m Ue ∈Rh×T .\r\nhe,t−1, se,t−1 and hd,t′−1, sd,t′−1 are the hidden states of\r\nthe encoder and decoder respectively. In both encoder and\r\ndecoder layer, we use m as the dimension of hidden states for\r\nsimplicity.\r\nThe output vector of the hybrid spatial attention layer is\r\npresented as: (a1\r\ntx1\r\nt, a2\r\ntx2\r\nt, ..., an\r\nt xn\r\nt )T ∈Rn. The aforemen-\r\ntioned output vector is processed by stacked LSTM layer to\r\nget the encoder output: Zt = (z1\r\nt , z2\r\nt , .., zm\r\nt )T ∈Rm×T . The\r\nencoder output serves as the input of the temporal attention\r\nlayer.\r\n4.2\r\nTemporal attention in decoder\r\nThe performance of the recurrent networks is limited by its\r\n’memory’. The LSTM units enhance the information stor-\r\nage over an extended time interval by using multiplicative\r\ngates to regulate the access to its memory [Hochreiter and\r\nSchmidhuber, 1997]. However, the LSTM-based networks\r\nstill has difficulty in capturing long-term dependency. Hence,\r\nby employing a temporal attention mechanism, the capability\r\nof capturing long-term dependency was maximised. Because\r\nthe selectively weighted hidden states along the sequence are\r\n\r\nDOCUMENT: P13_GAN_SSIM_2023_Figshare_copy.txt\r\nPAGE 19\r\n19\r\n\r\npredicted pressure is less than 𝑇𝑆𝑆𝐼𝑀𝑜 for 5 consecutive time steps). In practice, it is highly\r\n414\r\nfavorable to have the least DT to minimize water loss and its collateral damages. To quantify the\r\n415\r\naccuracy of leak localization, the graphical distance between the estimated leak location (i.e.\r\n416\r\nlocation with the minimum 𝑆𝑆𝐼𝑀𝑙), and the actual leak location, is employed. We denote this by\r\n417\r\nGDRL.\r\n418\r\n4.4.1. Hypothetical Single Leak Scenarios\r\n419\r\nFor the hypothetical single leaks, EPANET simulations are performed assuming that a single leak\r\n420\r\nhas occurred in the WDN. Three different leak rates (with leak areas (LAs) equal to 0.0005, 0.005,\r\n421\r\nand 0.05 m2), and eight alternative leak locations are simulated, resulting in a total of 24\r\n422\r\nhypothetical single leak scenarios. Two leaks are selected to represent each of the four leak-free\r\n423\r\npressure intervals defined in Fig. 7: one at the junction of 2 and the other at the junction of 3 pipes.\r\n424\r\nThe leak area (𝐴𝐿) is related to the leak flow rate (𝑞𝐿) through the following equation (Crowl and\r\n425\r\nLouvar, 2001):\r\n426\r\n𝑞𝐿= 𝐶𝐿𝐴𝐿√2𝑔ℎ𝐿\r\n(8)\r\nWhere 𝐶𝐿 is the discharge coefficient with a default value of 0.75, ℎ𝐿 is the head, and 𝑔 is the\r\n427\r\nacceleration of gravity. We assume that the leaks occur in a stepwise manner (with 10 equally\r\n428\r\nspaced steps) as demonstrated in Fig. 8, and are hence expected to cause a similarly stepwise\r\n429\r\npressure drop in parts of the network. The simulation period pertains to the WDN conditions in\r\n430\r\nthe first week of 2018, and the time steps are 5 minutes. For each scenario, this results in the\r\n431\r\ngeneration of 2,016 pairs of demand-pressure images. The generation of pressure images is once\r\n432\r\ndone using 780 observations, and then again for 33 OPs, to assess how the performance of the\r\n433\r\nproposed approach is affected by the number of observation locations. For all hypothetical\r\n434\r\nscenarios, the leak starts at 0:00 of day five and ends at 24:00 of the same day.\r\n435\r\n\r\n436\r\n\r\n437\r\nPAGE 20\r\n20\r\n\r\nLeak\r\nlocation*\r\nPressure\r\nRange\r\nJunction of\r\n2 pipes\r\n3 pipes\r\nP-40\r\n<30 m\r\n\r\n\r\nP-151\r\n\r\n\r\nP-151\r\n[30-40) m\r\n\r\n\r\nP-253\r\n\r\n\r\nP-880\r\n[40-50) m\r\n\r\n\r\nP 900\r\n\r\n\r\nP-224\r\n≥50 m\r\n\r\n\r\nP-888\r\n\r\n\r\n* The numbers represent the node numbers in the L-Town WDN (Vrachimis et al., 2020)\r\n438\r\nFig. 7. Hence, the model is perfectly capable of identifying\r\n460\r\nthis burst with minimal DT.\r\n461\r\nTo demonstrate how the proposed method identifies the leak location, Fig. 11 shows the 𝑆𝑆𝐼𝑀𝑙\r\n462\r\nmap for three different time steps during the leak. The location of the minimum 𝑆𝑆𝐼𝑀𝑙 and the\r\n463\r\nactual leak location is also shown in the figure. During the leak, 𝑆𝑆𝐼𝑀𝑙 significantly drops in the\r\n464\r\nvicinity of the leak location, forming a zone of low 𝑆𝑆𝐼𝑀𝑙 values around the leak location. The\r\n465\r\nextent of this zone increases with increasing leakage rates. The estimated location of the leak\r\n466\r\nPAGE 22\r\n22\r\n\r\n(pertaining to the minimum value of 𝑆𝑆𝐼𝑀𝑙) gradually converges to the actual leak location with\r\n467\r\nan increased leakage rate.
